kind=TEACH
title=Who owns it and which noun controls the form
body=The reflexive possessive refers to the subject as the owner. Its gender, number and case agree with the possessed noun, not with the owner. The retained dictionary forms are свой, своя, своё and свои. A masculine owner and a feminine owner can therefore use the same possessive form with the same noun.

kind=TEACH
title=The movie belongs to the subject
body=Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Borya told about his favorite movie.). А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Anna told about her favorite movie.). In each sentence the favorite movie belongs to the subject. The movie noun has the same gender, number and case in both, so the possessive form stays the same. The name and past-verb agreement change. The exercise asks you to preserve that movie object while changing the subject.

kind=TEACH
title=The same own book with two subjects
body=Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.

kind=TEACH
title=Plural jeans and the limits of the checks
body=Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы. — (Borya put on his own jeans.). А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы. — (Anna put on her own jeans.). Plural jeans require plural possessive agreement regardless of whether Borya or Anna owns them. The exercise keeps this object and changes the subject. The checks select supplied matched sentences; they do not demonstrate independently declining the possessive or constructing an ownership sentence.

title=Review the complete contrast
body=The reflexive possessive refers to the subject as the owner. Its gender, number and case agree with the possessed noun, not with the owner. The retained dictionary forms are свой, своя, своё and свои. A masculine owner and a feminine owner can therefore use the same possessive form with the same noun.

Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Borya told about his favorite movie.). А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Anna told about her favorite movie.). In each sentence the favorite movie belongs to the subject. The movie noun has the same gender, number and case in both, so the possessive form stays the same. The name and past-verb agreement change. The exercise asks you to preserve that movie object while changing the subject.

Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.

Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы. — (Borya put on his own jeans.). А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы. — (Anna put on her own jeans.). Plural jeans require plural possessive agreement regardless of whether Borya or Anna owns them. The exercise keeps this object and changes the subject. The checks select supplied matched sentences; they do not demonstrate independently declining the possessive or constructing an ownership sentence.

kind=TEACH
title=The person loved and the person who owns
body=Я люблю́ себя́ — I love myself. Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ — I love my wife. In the first sentence the object refers back to the subject. In the second the object is a wife; the parenthesized possessive makes ownership explicit. Loving oneself and loving a wife describe different objects.

- Content hash: eb12777f20c6b5332c6c7792798f73f5d6483caf364d2ccb3ca1be091868218b

kind=TEACH
title=A whole sentence is more than its object word
body=жену́ is the accusative form of the noun meaning wife. It does not mean I love. In Я люблю́ свою́ жену́ the verb supplies loving and the noun supplies its object. The full displayed sentence Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ means I love my wife; the full displayed sentence Я люблю́ себя́ means I love myself. Choose the whole-sentence meaning rather than translating the object word as the verb. These checks recognize the supplied love and wounded-object sentences; they do not demonstrate independently choosing every reflexive case form.

### STEP `u21-st-teach-object-reference-3`

- Objectives: obj-u21-object-reference

- Source: none

- Content hash: e97188e37e86230d624521db9585e3d45de62032104c34101938e1128b4d651e

kind=TEACH
title=Recognize the same-person object in a different sentence
body=Он поранил себя — He has wounded himself. Here the subject denotes the man and the direct object себя refers back to that same person. The verb and subject differ from guided Я люблю́ себя́. Compare the complete supplied Он поранил его — He has wounded him (someone else). In this contrast the other person is the object; the person wounded is different from the subject. In both wounded sentences the object is a person, not a possessed wife. In the retained Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ example, a wife is the object even when the possessive refers to the subject as owner. Keep object identity separate from ownership. These are supplied direct-object readings, not productive grammar, every pronoun case, emphasis or a rule that the reflexive-verb suffix always means accidental action.

- Content hash: 7d6ee160b662014f2502cab9c481ba17dc679292af18a018ed7327390a892540

kind=REMEDIATE
title=Review the complete contrast
body=Я люблю́ себя́ — I love myself. Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ — I love my wife. In the first sentence the object refers back to the subject. In the second the object is a wife; the parenthesized possessive makes ownership explicit. Loving oneself and loving a wife describe different objects.

жену́ is the accusative form of the noun meaning wife. It does not mean I love. In Я люблю́ свою́ жену́ the verb supplies loving and the noun supplies its object. The full displayed sentence Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ means I love my wife; the full displayed sentence Я люблю́ себя́ means I love myself. Choose the whole-sentence meaning rather than translating the object word as the verb. These checks recognize the supplied love and wounded-object sentences; they do not demonstrate independently choosing every reflexive case form.

Он поранил себя — He has wounded himself. Here the subject denotes the man and the direct object себя refers back to that same person. The verb and subject differ from guided Я люблю́ себя́. Compare the complete supplied Он поранил его — He has wounded him (someone else). In this contrast the other person is the object; the person wounded is different from the subject. In both wounded sentences the object is a person, not a possessed wife. In the retained Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ example, a wife is the object even when the possessive refers to the subject as owner. Keep object identity separate from ownership. These are supplied direct-object readings, not productive grammar, every pronoun case, emphasis or a rule that the reflexive-verb suffix always means accidental action.

kind=TEACH
title=Purpose and condition
body=чтобы — so that. если — if. The first conjunction can introduce a purpose; the second introduces a condition. In a purpose construction with the same subject for both actions, an infinitive after the purpose conjunction can name the intended action. This rule is explained here, but the current choices do not require composing such a sentence.

title=Ordinary purpose wording
body=чтобы is an ordinary purpose conjunction. The bookish or archaic qualifier in the retained source belongs to дабы, not to чтобы. The English meaning so that differs from the conditional if and the causal because. Select the meaning of the displayed conjunction; do not infer an archaic register for the ordinary form.

kind=TEACH
title=Cause, concession and time
body=потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. English since can also express a reason, so it is not a contrasting wrong answer to because in this exercise. The causal check instead contrasts because with although and after. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.

body=чтобы — so that. если — if. The first conjunction can introduce a purpose; the second introduces a condition. In a purpose construction with the same subject for both actions, an infinitive after the purpose conjunction can name the intended action. This rule is explained here, but the current choices do not require composing such a sentence.

чтобы is an ordinary purpose conjunction. The bookish or archaic qualifier in the retained source belongs to дабы, not to чтобы. The English meaning so that differs from the conditional if and the causal because. Select the meaning of the displayed conjunction; do not infer an archaic register for the ordinary form.

потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. English since can also express a reason, so it is not a contrasting wrong answer to because in this exercise. The causal check instead contrasts because with although and after. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.

title=Units 21–25 source notice
summary=Proposed Units 21–25 adapt admitted CC BY-SA 4.0 spans. Pressbooks evidence is separate from Wikimedia evidence. Bounded completion practice is review-pending, not full coverage.
detail=Wikibooks excerpts are frozen by oldid under the CC BY-SA 4.0 route recorded on Wikibooks:Copyrights oldid 4622060. The adapted selection and arrangement are share-alike. Application code, user data, and user recordings are outside that statement. No audio is included. Linked audio on the useful-words page is not admitted. Dated name-page claims about social address, foreigners, naming creativity, and the number of names are not shipped. English Wikipedia Russian grammar oldid 1369617627 is used under CC BY-SA 4.0 via Wikipedia:Copyrights oldid 1369466282. Russian Wikipedia pages are used under CC BY-SA 4.0 via the Russian Wikipedia copyrights page oldid 154562152. The one-and-a-half entry is frozen at English Wiktionary oldid 89737719 under CC BY-SA 4.0 via Wiktionary:Copyrights oldid 87545419. Linked audio is not admitted. Unit 23 time expressions are frozen Pressbooks chapter bytes sha256 87815b8724cb11aecba5bb6d8b6438f783408f2354e2560a3c0fe026fac1e171, retrieved 2026-09-26T22:52:41Z, under that chapter's own CC BY-SA 4.0 footer. That Pressbooks route is not a Wikipedia, Wiktionary, or Wikibooks license. Russian Wikipedia personal-name page oldid 154915954 is retained for bounded classification of the documented full-form and short-form diminutive bases, the dismissive-suffix limitation, and the lowered-status note, under the Russian Wikipedia copyrights page oldid 154562152. A separate application chain on that same span uses only names already in the span. It is not a name generator. The Unit 24 word-order pair is bounded sentence recognition on English Wikipedia Russian grammar oldid 1369617627. It does not clear scope-sensitive placement. Scope-sensitive placement is the ReadyRussian Language Generator 3 statement that the modifier negates the following word, and that page's two-sentence contrast, under the ReadyRussian CC BY-SA 4.0 route. Review is pending. Bounded practice for completion over a period is implemented and review-pending. It is not approved coverage of every temporal clause. Book work stays in the copy you own.

originalSourceText=Retained Wikipedia Russian grammar revision1369617627 possession/reflexive/conjunction spans. Cornell Beginning Russian Grammar le12_21_d.htm (subject reference) and gr11_c_1.htm (purpose with infinitive), https://russian.cornell.edu/grammar/html/, consulted2026-10-11. Independently authored English explanation; linguistic review pending.
Я люблю́ себя́ — I love myself. Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ — I love my wife. In the first sentence the object refers back to the subject. In the second the object is a wife; the parenthesized possessive makes ownership explicit. Loving oneself and loving a wife describe different objects.

жену́ is the accusative form of the noun meaning wife. It does not mean I love. In Я люблю́ свою́ жену́ the verb supplies loving and the noun supplies its object. The full displayed sentence Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ means I love my wife; the full displayed sentence Я люблю́ себя́ means I love myself. Choose the whole-sentence meaning rather than translating the object word as the verb. These checks recognize two retained sentences; they do not demonstrate independently choosing every reflexive case form.

Preserve the correct guided Я люблю́ себя́ / I love myself and independent Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ / I love my wife whole-object unchanged, all original examples and earlier native/source records. The changed independent check recognizes a different subject/action with a reflexive direct object; the contrasting supplied его sentence denotes someone else. Only these supplied object readings are taught; no universal all-case antecedent algorithm, emphasis rule, automatic/accidental -ся interpretation, all possessive style restrictions or whole-article grammar adoption. The source wording about emphasis and accidental reflexive verbs is explicitly excluded as unreliable beyond scope. All three teaching cards precede practice and full remediation is retained. Он поранил себя means He has wounded himself. The direct object себя refers back to the subject:the person wounded is the same person who wounded him. The contrasting source sentence Он поранил его has a non-reflexive pronoun referring to someone else. Read the whole supplied sentence; this does not test every reflexive case or the separate reflexive-verb interpretation.

Я люблю́ себя́ — I love myself. Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ — I love my wife. In the first sentence the object refers back to the subject. In the second the object is a wife; the parenthesized possessive makes ownership explicit. Loving oneself and loving a wife describe different objects.

The full displayed sentence Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ means I love my wife; the full displayed sentence Я люблю́ себя́ means I love myself. Choose the whole-sentence meaning rather than translating the object word as the verb. These checks recognize the supplied love and wounded-object sentences; they do not demonstrate independently choosing every reflexive case form.

Он поранил себя — He has wounded himself. Here the subject denotes the man and the direct object себя refers back to that same person. The verb and subject differ from guided Я люблю́ себя́. Compare the complete supplied Он поранил его — He has wounded him (someone else). In this contrast the other person is the object; the person wounded is different from the subject. In both wounded sentences the object is a person, not a possessed wife. In the retained Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ example, a wife is the object even when the possessive refers to the subject as owner. Keep object identity separate from ownership. These are supplied direct-object readings, not productive grammar, every pronoun case, emphasis or a rule that the reflexive-verb suffix always means accidental action.
shippedText=Я люблю́ себя́ — I love myself. Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ — I love my wife. In the first sentence the object refers back to the subject. In the second the object is a wife; the parenthesized possessive makes ownership explicit. Loving oneself and loving a wife describe different objects.

жену́ is the accusative form of the noun meaning wife. It does not mean I love. In Я люблю́ свою́ жену́ the verb supplies loving and the noun supplies its object. The full displayed sentence Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ means I love my wife; the full displayed sentence Я люблю́ себя́ means I love myself. Choose the whole-sentence meaning rather than translating the object word as the verb. These checks recognize the supplied love and wounded-object sentences; they do not demonstrate independently choosing every reflexive case form.

In both wounded sentences the object is a person, not a possessed wife. In the retained Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ example, a wife is the object even when the possessive refers to the subject as owner. Keep object identity separate from ownership. These are supplied direct-object readings, not productive grammar, every pronoun case, emphasis or a rule that the reflexive-verb suffix always means accidental action.

Complete raw context:
=== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}} {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} universally means "oneself"/"myself"/"himself", etc. It is inflected depending on the [[Grammatical case|case]].<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=111}}</ref>

When used to indicate that the person is the direct object of the verb, one uses the [[Accusative case|accusative]] form, {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}}.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=98}}</ref> (It does not have a nominative form.)

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil sebya}}. ("He has wounded himself.")

Emphasized forms are "sam sebya" - masculine, "sama sebya" - feminine, "sami sebya" - plural. Unless there is a particular reason (emphasis, answering a question) the word "sam" usually comes after the noun it is emphasizing.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=121}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|'''Он''' сам поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On sam poranil sebya}}. ("'''He''' has wounded himself." Literally: "He himself has wounded himself.")
However the form, e.g.,  {{lang|ru|сам себя '''oн''' поранил}}, is grammatical as well.

This sentence underlines that the subject inflicted the wounds while in the previous example, "sebya" merely indicates that the subject was wounded.

The reflexive pronoun {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} gave rise the reflexive affix {{lang|ru-latn|-sya}} ({{lang|ru|-ся}}) used to generate [[reflexive verb]]s, but in this context the affix indicates that the action happened accidentally:<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=175}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он поранился}} (He has wounded himself.)

There are stylistic differences between the three usages, despite being rendered in the same way in English.

Russian has a reflexive possessive as well.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=82–83}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он любит свою жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit svoyu zhenu.}} (''He loves his wife (his own).'' - Reflexive possessive)
* {{lang|ru|Он любит его жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit yego zhenu}} (''He loves his wife (someone else's).'' - It is ambiguous in English, but less so in Russian.)

Because of the existence of reflexive forms, the use of a non-reflexive pronoun indicates a subject that is different from the object. If it is impossible, the sentence is invalid or at least irregular:

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил его}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil ego}}. ("He has wounded him (someone else).")
Context explanation:
Он поранил себя means He has wounded himself. The direct object себя refers back to the subject:the person wounded is the same person who wounded him. The contrasting source sentence Он поранил его has a non-reflexive pronoun referring to someone else. Read the whole supplied sentence; this does not test every reflexive case or the separate reflexive-verb interpretation.
Tuple-v1:tuple-v1:1;28:Он поранил себя23:He has wounded himself.1;34:He has wounded him (someone else).

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08

sourceRecordId=ca-rule-context:u21-i-object-reference-2
originalSourceText=English Wikipedia Reflexive pronoun1374712982, complete Russian section:exact direct-object sentences Он поранил себя / Он поранил его and supplied English meanings. Retained pre2014 revision586336289 (2013-12-16) independently documents both exact native examples and meanings before the current section cites a book dated2014; that later citation is not the source basis for this bounded intake, and no book prose is adopted. Exact API SHA1/size/SHA256/raw responses/history are retained. First-party Wikipedia text selection/arrangement is CC BY-SA4.0 via actual retained Wikipedia:Copyrights1369466282, stable oldid/history/contributor attribution. No blanket hosting-license or qualified rights approval; historical chronology is not a legal clearance. Only literal template language values are selected, with no native generation, stress addition, punctuation repair, private Penguin prose or media. Preserve the correct guided Я люблю́ себя́ / I love myself and independent Я люблю́ (свою́) жену́ / I love my wife whole-object unchanged, all original examples and earlier native/source records. The changed independent check recognizes a different subject/action with a reflexive direct object; the contrasting supplied его sentence denotes someone else. Only these supplied object readings are taught; no universal all-case antecedent algorithm, emphasis rule, automatic/accidental -ся interpretation, all possessive style restrictions or whole-article grammar adoption. The source wording about emphasis and accidental reflexive verbs is explicitly excluded as unreliable beyond scope. All three teaching cards precede practice and full remediation is retained. Independently authored English2026-10-13 within content-audit-2026-10-08; qualified source/rights/linguistic acceptance pending.
Complete raw context:
=== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}} {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} universally means "oneself"/"myself"/"himself", etc. It is inflected depending on the [[Grammatical case|case]].<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=111}}</ref>

When used to indicate that the person is the direct object of the verb, one uses the [[Accusative case|accusative]] form, {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}}.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=98}}</ref> (It does not have a nominative form.)

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil sebya}}. ("He has wounded himself.")

Emphasized forms are "sam sebya" - masculine, "sama sebya" - feminine, "sami sebya" - plural. Unless there is a particular reason (emphasis, answering a question) the word "sam" usually comes after the noun it is emphasizing.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=121}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|'''Он''' сам поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On sam poranil sebya}}. ("'''He''' has wounded himself." Literally: "He himself has wounded himself.")
However the form, e.g.,  {{lang|ru|сам себя '''oн''' поранил}}, is grammatical as well.

This sentence underlines that the subject inflicted the wounds while in the previous example, "sebya" merely indicates that the subject was wounded.

The reflexive pronoun {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} gave rise the reflexive affix {{lang|ru-latn|-sya}} ({{lang|ru|-ся}}) used to generate [[reflexive verb]]s, but in this context the affix indicates that the action happened accidentally:<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=175}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он поранился}} (He has wounded himself.)

There are stylistic differences between the three usages, despite being rendered in the same way in English.

Russian has a reflexive possessive as well.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=82–83}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он любит свою жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit svoyu zhenu.}} (''He loves his wife (his own).'' - Reflexive possessive)
* {{lang|ru|Он любит его жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit yego zhenu}} (''He loves his wife (someone else's).'' - It is ambiguous in English, but less so in Russian.)

Because of the existence of reflexive forms, the use of a non-reflexive pronoun indicates a subject that is different from the object. If it is impossible, the sentence is invalid or at least irregular:

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил его}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil ego}}. ("He has wounded him (someone else).")
Context explanation:
Он поранил себя means He has wounded himself. The direct object себя refers back to the subject:the person wounded is the same person who wounded him. The contrasting source sentence Он поранил его has a non-reflexive pronoun referring to someone else. Read the whole supplied sentence; this does not test every reflexive case or the separate reflexive-verb interpretation.
Tuple-v1:tuple-v1:1;28:Он поранил себя23:He has wounded himself.1;34:He has wounded him (someone else).

### PROVENANCE `prov-ca-u21-purpose-rem-body`

- Objectives: obj-u21-purpose

- Content hash: a21603274115319b1f3dbe7f0eca66937ea2d64afce7046b7aba3c3adfc14dfb

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u21-purpose:purpose-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikipedia Russian grammar revision1369617627 possession/reflexive/conjunction spans. Cornell Beginning Russian Grammar le12_21_d.htm (subject reference) and gr11_c_1.htm (purpose with infinitive), https://russian.cornell.edu/grammar/html/, consulted2026-10-11. Independently authored English explanation; linguistic review pending.
чтобы — so that. если — if. The first conjunction can introduce a purpose; the second introduces a condition. In a purpose construction with the same subject for both actions, an infinitive after the purpose conjunction can name the intended action. This rule is explained here, but the current choices do not require composing such a sentence.

чтобы is an ordinary purpose conjunction. The bookish or archaic qualifier in the retained source belongs to дабы, not to чтобы. The English meaning so that differs from the conditional if and the causal because. Select the meaning of the displayed conjunction; do not infer an archaic register for the ordinary form.

потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. English since can also express a reason, so it is not a contrasting wrong answer to because in this exercise. The causal check instead contrasts because with although and after. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.
shippedText=чтобы — so that. если — if. The first conjunction can introduce a purpose; the second introduces a condition. In a purpose construction with the same subject for both actions, an infinitive after the purpose conjunction can name the intended action. This rule is explained here, but the current choices do not require composing such a sentence.

чтобы is an ordinary purpose conjunction. The bookish or archaic qualifier in the retained source belongs to дабы, not to чтобы. The English meaning so that differs from the conditional if and the causal because. Select the meaning of the displayed conjunction; do not infer an archaic register for the ordinary form.

потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. English since can also express a reason, so it is not a contrasting wrong answer to because in this exercise. The causal check instead contrasts because with although and after. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.

originalSourceText=Retained Wikipedia Russian grammar revision1369617627 possession/reflexive/conjunction spans. Cornell Beginning Russian Grammar le12_21_d.htm (subject reference) and gr11_c_1.htm (purpose with infinitive), https://russian.cornell.edu/grammar/html/, consulted2026-10-11. Independently authored English explanation; linguistic review pending.
потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. English since can also express a reason, so it is not a contrasting wrong answer to because in this exercise. The causal check instead contrasts because with although and after. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.
shippedText=потому что — because. хотя — although. The first gives a reason; the second introduces a concession. The English label after names a temporal relationship rather than a reason. These conjunction meanings are distinct from purpose and condition.

### PROVENANCE `prov-ca-u21-svoy-rem-body`

- Objectives: obj-u21-svoy
- Sessions: u21-s-orient, u21-s-svoy
- Source: src-content-audit-research
- Rights: rights-content-audit-research
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: 13391192f4891cea78e430e9e5bd278eda3ad8d730314be42effc9c41e365ea7

recordType=label
sourceUrl=urn:russian-companion:content-audit-research:2026-10-08
sourceRevision=content-audit-2026-10-08
sourceRecordId=ca-coverage:obj-u21-svoy:svoy-rem-body
originalSourceText=Retained Wikipedia Russian grammar revision1369617627 possession/reflexive/conjunction spans. Cornell Beginning Russian Grammar le12_21_d.htm (subject reference) and gr11_c_1.htm (purpose with infinitive), https://russian.cornell.edu/grammar/html/, consulted2026-10-11. Independently authored English explanation; linguistic review pending.
The reflexive possessive refers to the subject as the owner. Its gender, number and case agree with the possessed noun, not with the owner. The retained dictionary forms are свой, своя, своё and свои. A masculine owner and a feminine owner can therefore use the same possessive form with the same noun.

Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Borya told about his favorite movie.). А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Anna told about her favorite movie.). In each sentence the favorite movie belongs to the subject. The movie noun has the same gender, number and case in both, so the possessive form stays the same. The name and past-verb agreement change. The exercise asks you to preserve that movie object while changing the subject.

Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.

Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы. — (Borya put on his own jeans.). А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы. — (Anna put on her own jeans.). Plural jeans require plural possessive agreement regardless of whether Borya or Anna owns them. The exercise keeps this object and changes the subject. The checks select supplied matched sentences; they do not demonstrate independently declining the possessive or constructing an ownership sentence.
shippedText=The reflexive possessive refers to the subject as the owner. Its gender, number and case agree with the possessed noun, not with the owner. The retained dictionary forms are свой, своя, своё and свои. A masculine owner and a feminine owner can therefore use the same possessive form with the same noun.

Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Borya told about his favorite movie.). А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме. — (Anna told about her favorite movie.). In each sentence the favorite movie belongs to the subject. The movie noun has the same gender, number and case in both, so the possessive form stays the same. The name and past-verb agreement change. The exercise asks you to preserve that movie object while changing the subject.

Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.

Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы. — (Borya put on his own jeans.). А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы. — (Anna put on her own jeans.). Plural jeans require plural possessive agreement regardless of whether Borya or Anna owns them. The exercise keeps this object and changes the subject. The checks select supplied matched sentences; they do not demonstrate independently declining the possessive or constructing an ownership sentence.

originalSourceText=Retained Wikipedia Russian grammar revision1369617627 possession/reflexive/conjunction spans. Cornell Beginning Russian Grammar le12_21_d.htm (subject reference) and gr11_c_1.htm (purpose with infinitive), https://russian.cornell.edu/grammar/html/, consulted2026-10-11. Independently authored English explanation; linguistic review pending.
Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.
shippedText=Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла. — (She never wrote her own book.). Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л. — (He never wrote his own book.). The possessive agrees with the feminine singular book noun; the shown form is genitive in this negative sentence. Changing the subject changes the past verb's agreement, not the book phrase. These sentences are about a book, not a movie or jeans.

Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы. — (Borya put on his own jeans.). А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы. — (Anna put on her own jeans.). Plural jeans require plural possessive agreement regardless of whether Borya or Anna owns them. The exercise keeps this object and changes the subject. The checks select supplied matched sentences; they do not demonstrate independently declining the possessive or constructing an ownership sentence.

* после того, как {{IPA|[ˈposʲlʲɪ tɐˈvo kək]}} 'after'
* хотя {{IPA|[xɐˈtʲa]}} 'although'

- Content hash: d07c42c558bec43a3d8bb52c5a6a3d5a01967df3ace441c7d0e2db2584b17331

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Possessive_Pronouns?oldid=4220315
sourceRevision=4220315
sourceRecordId=possessive:The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё h
originalSourceText=The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё his/her/its own n., свои his/her/its own pl.), as well as all possessive nouns except the 3rd person ones take the gender and the quantity of the object in possession:

:"фильм" (movie) is of masculine gender, and:
::Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Borya told about his favorite movie.)
::А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Anna told about her favorite movie.)

::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, Бо́ря."  (I didn't see your favorite movie, Borya).
::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, А́нна." (I didn't see your favorite movie, Anna.)

:"кни́га" (book) is of feminine gender, and:
::Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л.  (He never wrote his own book.)
::Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла.  (She never wrote her own book.)

::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, Бо́ря?"  (Can I take your book, Borya?)
::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, А́нна?"  (Can I take your book, Anna?)

:"джи́нсы" (jeans) is a plural-only noun, and:
::Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы.  (Borya put on his own jeans.)
::А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы.  (Anna put on her own jeans.)

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Possessive_Pronouns?oldid=4220315
sourceRevision=4220315
sourceRecordId=possessive:The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё h
originalSourceText=The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё his/her/its own n., свои his/her/its own pl.), as well as all possessive nouns except the 3rd person ones take the gender and the quantity of the object in possession:

:"фильм" (movie) is of masculine gender, and:
::Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Borya told about his favorite movie.)
::А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Anna told about her favorite movie.)

::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, Бо́ря."  (I didn't see your favorite movie, Borya).
::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, А́нна." (I didn't see your favorite movie, Anna.)

:"кни́га" (book) is of feminine gender, and:
::Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л.  (He never wrote his own book.)
::Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла.  (She never wrote her own book.)

::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, Бо́ря?"  (Can I take your book, Borya?)
::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, А́нна?"  (Can I take your book, Anna?)

:"джи́нсы" (jeans) is a plural-only noun, and:
::Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы.  (Borya put on his own jeans.)
::А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы.  (Anna put on her own jeans.)
shippedText=(Borya put on his own jeans.)

- Objectives: obj-u21-object-reference
- Sessions: u21-s-orient, u21-s-object-reference
- Source: src-enwiki-reflexive-pronoun-1374712982
- Rights: rights-enwiki-reflexive-pronoun-1374712982
- Classification: none
- Current review status: IMPLEMENTED_PENDING_FINAL_AUDIT
- Content hash: b1265d009b1be25a9db2644b0789cb8aad27090d0ee2d477d133246ab56776d9

recordType=example
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reflexive_pronoun&oldid=1374712982
sourceRevision=1374712982
sourceRecordId=reflexive-independent-direct-object:=== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}
originalSourceText==== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}} {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} universally means "oneself"/"myself"/"himself", etc. It is inflected depending on the [[Grammatical case|case]].<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=111}}</ref>

When used to indicate that the person is the direct object of the verb, one uses the [[Accusative case|accusative]] form, {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}}.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=98}}</ref> (It does not have a nominative form.)

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil sebya}}. ("He has wounded himself.")

Emphasized forms are "sam sebya" - masculine, "sama sebya" - feminine, "sami sebya" - plural. Unless there is a particular reason (emphasis, answering a question) the word "sam" usually comes after the noun it is emphasizing.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=121}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|'''Он''' сам поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On sam poranil sebya}}. ("'''He''' has wounded himself." Literally: "He himself has wounded himself.")
However the form, e.g.,  {{lang|ru|сам себя '''oн''' поранил}}, is grammatical as well.

This sentence underlines that the subject inflicted the wounds while in the previous example, "sebya" merely indicates that the subject was wounded.

The reflexive pronoun {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} gave rise the reflexive affix {{lang|ru-latn|-sya}} ({{lang|ru|-ся}}) used to generate [[reflexive verb]]s, but in this context the affix indicates that the action happened accidentally:<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=175}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он поранился}} (He has wounded himself.)

There are stylistic differences between the three usages, despite being rendered in the same way in English.

Russian has a reflexive possessive as well.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=82–83}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он любит свою жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit svoyu zhenu.}} (''He loves his wife (his own).'' - Reflexive possessive)
* {{lang|ru|Он любит его жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit yego zhenu}} (''He loves his wife (someone else's).'' - It is ambiguous in English, but less so in Russian.)

Because of the existence of reflexive forms, the use of a non-reflexive pronoun indicates a subject that is different from the object. If it is impossible, the sentence is invalid or at least irregular:

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил его}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil ego}}. ("He has wounded him (someone else).")

sourceRevision=1374712982
sourceRecordId=reflexive-independent-direct-object:=== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}
originalSourceText==== Russian ===

In [[Russian Language|Russian]], the pronoun {{lang|ru|себя}} {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} universally means "oneself"/"myself"/"himself", etc. It is inflected depending on the [[Grammatical case|case]].<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=111}}</ref>

When used to indicate that the person is the direct object of the verb, one uses the [[Accusative case|accusative]] form, {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}}.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=98}}</ref> (It does not have a nominative form.)

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil sebya}}. ("He has wounded himself.")

Emphasized forms are "sam sebya" - masculine, "sama sebya" - feminine, "sami sebya" - plural. Unless there is a particular reason (emphasis, answering a question) the word "sam" usually comes after the noun it is emphasizing.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=121}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|'''Он''' сам поранил себя}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On sam poranil sebya}}. ("'''He''' has wounded himself." Literally: "He himself has wounded himself.")
However the form, e.g.,  {{lang|ru|сам себя '''oн''' поранил}}, is grammatical as well.

This sentence underlines that the subject inflicted the wounds while in the previous example, "sebya" merely indicates that the subject was wounded.

The reflexive pronoun {{lang|ru-latn|sebya}} gave rise the reflexive affix {{lang|ru-latn|-sya}} ({{lang|ru|-ся}}) used to generate [[reflexive verb]]s, but in this context the affix indicates that the action happened accidentally:<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=175}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он поранился}} (He has wounded himself.)

There are stylistic differences between the three usages, despite being rendered in the same way in English.

Russian has a reflexive possessive as well.<ref>{{Cite book|title=Морфология простым языком|last=Naumanova|first=Irina|publisher=Unknown|year=2014|location=Moscow|pages=82–83}}</ref>

* {{lang|ru|Он любит свою жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit svoyu zhenu.}} (''He loves his wife (his own).'' - Reflexive possessive)
* {{lang|ru|Он любит его жену.}} {{lang|ru-latn|On lyubit yego zhenu}} (''He loves his wife (someone else's).'' - It is ambiguous in English, but less so in Russian.)

Because of the existence of reflexive forms, the use of a non-reflexive pronoun indicates a subject that is different from the object. If it is impossible, the sentence is invalid or at least irregular:

* {{lang|ru|Он поранил его}}. {{lang|ru-latn|On poranil ego}}. ("He has wounded him (someone else).")

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Possessive_Pronouns?oldid=4220315
sourceRevision=4220315
sourceRecordId=possessive:The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё h
originalSourceText=The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё his/her/its own n., свои his/her/its own pl.), as well as all possessive nouns except the 3rd person ones take the gender and the quantity of the object in possession:

:"фильм" (movie) is of masculine gender, and:
::Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Borya told about his favorite movie.)
::А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Anna told about her favorite movie.)

::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, Бо́ря."  (I didn't see your favorite movie, Borya).
::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, А́нна." (I didn't see your favorite movie, Anna.)

:"кни́га" (book) is of feminine gender, and:
::Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л.  (He never wrote his own book.)
::Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла.  (She never wrote her own book.)

::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, Бо́ря?"  (Can I take your book, Borya?)
::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, А́нна?"  (Can I take your book, Anna?)

:"джи́нсы" (jeans) is a plural-only noun, and:
::Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы.  (Borya put on his own jeans.)
::А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы.  (Anna put on her own jeans.)
shippedText=The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё his/her/its own n., свои his/her/its own pl.), as well as all possessive nouns except the 3rd person ones take the gender and the quantity of the object in possession:

sourceRecordId=possessive:The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё h
originalSourceText=The reflexive pronouns (свой his/her/its own m., своя his/her/its own f., своё his/her/its own n., свои his/her/its own pl.), as well as all possessive nouns except the 3rd person ones take the gender and the quantity of the object in possession:

:"фильм" (movie) is of masculine gender, and:
::Бо́ря рассказа́л о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Borya told about his favorite movie.)
::А́нна рассказа́ла о своём люби́мом фи́льме.  (Anna told about her favorite movie.)

::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, Бо́ря."  (I didn't see your favorite movie, Borya).
::"Я не ви́дел твоего́ люби́мого фи́льма, А́нна." (I didn't see your favorite movie, Anna.)

:"кни́га" (book) is of feminine gender, and:
::Свое́й кни́ги он никогда́ не написа́л.  (He never wrote his own book.)
::Свое́й кни́ги она́ никогда́ не написа́ла.  (She never wrote her own book.)

::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, Бо́ря?"  (Can I take your book, Borya?)
::"Мо́жно, возьму́ твою́ кни́гу, А́нна?"  (Can I take your book, Anna?)

:"джи́нсы" (jeans) is a plural-only noun, and:
::Бо́ря наде́л свои́ джи́нсы.  (Borya put on his own jeans.)
::А́нна наде́ла свои́ джи́нсы.  (Anna put on her own jeans.)
